Data mining 2026 · the deep read · first principles

From a tangled web to one true answer.

All 1487 technical papers of WWW, WSDM and SIGIR 2026, arranged not as a list of topics but as one story — the journey the field takes to connect a person to information, from organizing the raw chaos, through search and recommendation, to language models that read and answer, all while guarding against fraud, bias, and lies, and making it all run for billions at once.

How do you connect a person to the right information — and, increasingly, the right answer — out of a web too vast to read and too unreliable to trust?

That's the whole of data mining and web information systems on one line. Everything below is a piece of the answer. The field has six jobs: first it must organize what exists into something machine-readable; then it must handle the daily acts of search and recommendation; increasingly it understands and answers in natural language; it has to guard against abuse, fraud, bias, and misinformation; and it must do all of this at scale. Six stages, one pipeline — and this year, a single new thread (large language models, in all their forms) running through nearly every one.

I. Organize what's knownII. Find the needleIII. Anticipate the wantIV. Understand and answerV. Guard the commonsVI. Do it at scale
Stage I · 242 papers

Organize what's known

The problem. The web isn't a library with a tidy catalog; it's a churning, unlabeled tangle — billions of pages, clicks, purchases, and connections, with no built-in structure. Before you can find or recommend or reason about any of it, you have to turn that mess into something a computer can work with: a map of what exists and how it all connects. The hard part is that the connections are the meaning — who links to whom, what's bought with what, which fact relates to which — and there are far too many to write down by hand.

The approach. Represent everything as relationships. Store the world as a giant web of things and the links between them, and learn a compact string of numbers for each item so that similar things end up near each other. Special care goes to the shape of the connections themselves — friend networks, webs of facts — because how things are wired together carries as much meaning as the things being wired.

Graph Learning & Mining 55

Problem. Much of the world is a web of connections — friends, links, purchases — where the connections themselves carry the meaning, but ordinary tools expect neat rows and columns, not tangles. Approach. Learn directly on the network, letting each thing's meaning be shaped by its neighbors, so the structure becomes something you can compute with and predict from.

Long-Tailed Recognition of Evidential Experts for Graph-level Classification
WWW
problem Graph classification systems fail on rare categories because they train on balanced data and become biased toward common categories, plus they can't admit when they're uncertain.
approach Use multiple experts with evidence-based beliefs (modeled as probability distributions) and uncertainty estimates, so the system combines their views robustly for both common and rare categories.
Topology-Aware Feature Sorting Enables Universal Modeling on Homophilic and Heterophilic Graphs
WWW
problem A system that learns from how things connect works best when connected items are similar, but it breaks on networks where connected items are often different.
approach Look at connections between individual features—not just whole items—to adapt to networks where similar things connect or opposite things connect equally often.
Concordia: Enabling Low-Conflict Distributed Transaction Scheduling in Sharding Blockchain via Cooperative Perception
WWW
problem Blockchain networks split work across many shards for speed, but when a transaction touches multiple shards, they conflict and slow things down dramatically.
approach Each shard predicts which other shards it will interact with soon and shares simple hints, so all shards can schedule their work together.
LEDA: Latent Semantic Distribution Alignment for Multi-domain Graph Pre-training
WWW
problem Training one model to work across wildly different graph databases fails because the data types and structures are completely different between domains.
approach Project all domains into a shared semantic space by learning what features matter in each, then use a shared hidden distribution to guide training.
MultiFPT: Towards Multi-Attribute Fairness in Pre-Trained Graph Neural Networks via Prompt Tuning
WWW
problem Pre-trained AI systems that learn from networks often discriminate against people with multiple overlapping identities (like race plus gender), but fixing this requires retraining the whole system which costs too much.
approach Add small adjustable components to the pre-trained system and inject fairness hints at the start, tuning only those small pieces to reduce bias against underrepresented groups while keeping the main system frozen.

WWW
problem Detecting how groups evolve in networks is crucial for safety in hospitals or traffic, but today's systems are black boxes that give no explanation for why groups formed.

WWW
problem Graph-learning systems work well in training but fail suddenly when data shifts slightly, giving wrong answers where they used to give right ones.
approach Study how the loss landscape becomes sharper during training and boost generalization by augmenting data in ways that prevent answers from flipping under small changes.

WWW
problem When different offices cluster the same data privately, they disagree on how many clusters exist, breaking the ability to combine their results fairly.
approach Separate each office's data into public patterns everyone agrees on and private details that stay local, then align only the public parts.

WWW
problem Networks of connected data (like social graphs) confuse AI systems when friends aren't similar, and systems that work on training data fail when test data has different connection patterns.
approach Create a codebook of connection patterns during training, then during testing combine them flexibly to handle rare or new patterns the model never saw.

A Unified Graph Clustering Network
WWW
problem Graph-based clustering methods treat node-level and graph-level grouping as separate problems, missing chances to use insights from one level to improve the other and wasting compute.
approach Learn both node and graph clusters at the same time in two connected branches that push the same information back and forth, so local patterns guide global grouping and vice versa.

WWW
problem Classifying nodes in networks fails when test data contains completely new classes never seen during training, and models cannot cleanly separate known from unknown categories.
approach Mix training data with neighboring patterns in strategic ways and use cross-layer similarity matching to make the model tighter within classes and more confident rejecting unknowns.

WWW
problem AI systems trained for one kind of network graph (like social networks) fail on another kind (like molecule structures) because features mean totally different things in each domain.
approach They identified what stays the same about node features across all graphs and trained a foundation model that transforms any graph into a shared format for knowledge transfer.
Difference-based Sample Selection for Federated Graph Rationalization
WWW
problem When companies learn together without sharing raw data, their models latch onto local quirks instead of real patterns, and we can't tell which pieces of the model actually matter.
approach Find training examples where the models disagree, use those to fix shortcut learning, and add noise to features to make predictions depend only on true patterns.
Explainable Graph Sparsification with Shapley Values
WWW
problem Graph systems often remove edges to go faster, but nobody explains which edges are useless and which are just quiet—you're guessing in the dark.
approach Use a fair scoring method to rank edges by importance, then remove the lowest ones, so you see exactly what was cut and why.
Text-attributed Graph Condensation via Text Selection and Attribute Matching
WWW
problem Graphs where nodes have text descriptions (like social media or research papers) take huge memory and time to train, slowing down AI research.
approach Compress both the text and connections by picking key text pieces and matching similar connections, shrinking datasets to 1% size without losing learning accuracy.

WWW

WWW
problem Real-world graphs have both known and unknown shifts in patterns, making models fail on new data, and fixing one problem often breaks the other solution.

E2SGNN: Reconciling Expression and Efficiency in Spiking Graph Neural Network
WWW
problem Mimicking the brain's efficient spiking neurons for graph problems lets systems run faster, but binary spiking is too weak to express nuance, so existing work adds expensive operations that kill the efficiency gain.

WWW

WWW
problem When a system that learns from how things connect has no raw data to work with, it can't tell two identical-looking nodes apart, leaving it blind.

WSDM

Addressing Aggregation-Induced Information Loss in Graph Neural Networks
WSDM
problem Systems that learn from how things connect (e.g., friends-of-friends networks) lose important information when they combine each person's data with their neighbors' data.
approach Before combining, diversify each person's features so they don't blend into mush, then save structural details afterward so neighborhood relationships stay visible.

Knowledge Graphs 36

Problem. Facts scattered through text are useless to a machine until they're organized as explicit, connected statements it can look up and reason over — and the world's facts are vast, messy, and always changing. Approach. Build a structured web of things and the relationships between them, and learn to fill in, correct, and query it as new facts arrive.

Self-Supervised & Contrastive 26

Problem. Powerful models are hungry for labeled examples, but labeling is slow and costly, and almost all real data arrives with no labels at all. Approach. Let the data teach itself — hide part of it and have the model predict the rest, or learn what counts as similar and different — building rich understanding from raw, unlabeled material.

Stage II · 180 papers

Find the needle

The problem. Once the world is organized, the everyday act is search: a person has a need, and somewhere in billions of items sit the few that answer it. This is harder than it sounds — the words a person uses rarely match the words in the answer, what counts as relevant depends on who's asking, and the whole thing has to come back in a fraction of a second.

The approach. Match by meaning, not just by words: turn both the question and every candidate into those number-strings and find the nearest ones, then carefully re-order the survivors so the best rises to the top. Much of this year's work is making that matching sharper and the ordering fairer and faster.

Information Retrieval & Search 180

Problem. A person has a need, and somewhere in billions of documents sit the few that answer it — but their words rarely match the document's, and the answer has to come back instantly. Approach. Compare by meaning rather than exact words, then carefully order the survivors so the most useful one rises to the top.

problem Building a working conversational search system requires connecting many different pieces of code, but researchers have no shared framework to build and share these components with the community.
approach Release an open platform with 45 ready-made modules for understanding questions, ranking results, and generating answers, so researchers can quickly assemble and test full systems.
Refairmulate: A Large-Scale Dataset for Gender-Fair Query Reformulations
SIGIR
problem Search engines sometimes return better results for people of certain genders and worse results for others, reinforcing unfair stereotypes baked into training data.
approach Created a dataset of search queries reworded to both find better answers AND reduce gender bias, then trained models to learn both goals at once.
SQuTR: A Robustness Benchmark for Spoken Query to Text Retrieval under Acoustic Noise
SIGIR
problem Voice search doesn't work well when there's background noise like traffic or crowds, but most tests use quiet conditions that don't reflect real usage.
approach Created a test collection mixing real office, street, and construction noise at different volumes with queries in English and Chinese to measure how systems handle messy real-world sound.
A Sensitivity-Aware Test Collection for Search Among Personal Information
SIGIR
problem Email searches return sensitive personal information people never wanted found, but we need to build search systems that balance usefulness with privacy.
approach Took a real email collection with manually marked sensitive content, crowdsourced test queries, and created a benchmark to measure how well systems find helpful information while hiding private details.
A Taxonomy and Catalog of SERP Elements Across Web Search Engines
SIGIR
problem Search engines now show many more things than just links—ads, maps, news—but there's no standard way to talk about or study these interface pieces across different search engines.
approach Examined Google, Bing, Yandex, and others to find all the different things they display, gave them consistent names, and organized them into a shared catalog for researchers.
NERBench-Chhattisgarh: A Multi-Family NER Dataset for Low-Resource Indic Languages
SIGIR
problem Tribal languages in India have almost no text on the web or in training data, so search systems can't find names and places people care about in those languages.
approach Manually annotated 166,000 words in seven tribal languages to mark names and places, then tested how well multilingual AI systems could learn from this small dataset with special techniques.
Content-Based Dataset Knowledge Graphs for Dataset Search
SIGIR
problem Finding useful datasets is hard because search only looks at file names and descriptions, ignoring the actual data inside and how datasets relate to each other.
approach Built a system that automatically pulls connections from both metadata and the actual contents of different data files, then ranks datasets by these enriched relationships.
MIRA: An LLM-Assisted Benchmark for Multi-Category Integrated Retrieval
SIGIR
problem Modern search engines must find four types of things at once—papers, datasets, tools, variables—but we lack proper test sets for this unified search.
approach Created a test set from real searches on a social science platform, covering all four item types, using AI to speed up labeling.
IIRSim Studio: A Dashboard for User Simulation
SIGIR
problem Simulating user behavior is valuable for testing search engines cheaply, but it requires heavy coding, blocking most researchers from using it.
approach Built a web interface where you design and run user simulations visually, without writing code, storing experiments as reusable templates.
SimEval-IR: A Unified Toolkit and Benchmark Suite for Evaluating User Simulators and Search Sessions
SIGIR
problem User simulators can look human-like but give wrong answers about whether one search system actually ranks better than another.
approach Built standard tests showing that looking human-like has almost no connection to valid system rankings, pinpointing better realism measures.
Does LLM Relevance Labelling Work for Arabic?
SIGIR
problem Arabic search systems lack evaluation resources; we need to know if AI can reliably rate Arabic search results like it does English.
approach Tested whether three AI systems agree on Arabic search result quality and whether their ratings match real system rankings across languages.
The Matryoshka Hypencoder
SIGIR
problem Some retrieval methods are smart but slow; search services need fast scoring without losing accuracy.
approach Modified a retrieval method to run at multiple speed-accuracy points, achieving 7x fewer computations without major quality loss.
Modalities of Expert Search: How Users Transition Between Names and Topics
SIGIR
problem People search for experts both by name and by skill area, switching modes as they learn; we don't know how to support this well.
approach Analyzed half a million real expert search queries, finding that people switch modes when they find something, reshaping search design.

SIGIR
problem Millions of people describe their mental health struggles online, but doctors can't manually read all of it. Systems need to spot depression symptoms buried in posts to help overworked clinics.
approach Instead of guessing what counts as depression evidence, feed an AI system real labeled examples to learn brief rules about each symptom, then use those rules to flag relevant posts.
Load-sensitive Selective Pruning in Dense Retrieval
SIGIR
problem Search engines must balance speed and accuracy: exact search works best but is slow under load. When busy, they must cut corners without losing users' results.
approach Shrink the search data when traffic spikes and grow it back when quiet, automatically picking the right size to hit response deadlines.
Scalable K-Means Guided Partitioning for Block-based Sparse Document Retrieval
SIGIR
problem Libraries with millions of books need to group similar ones together to search fast, but the standard grouping method gets too slow as the library grows.
approach Use a faster splitting strategy that groups books almost as well as before but runs hundreds of times quicker, even on the largest libraries.
From Continuous Pretraining to Domain-Adaptive Reranking via Task Vector Adaptation
SIGIR
problem Teaching an AI to rank results in a medical database costs huge effort to build training data. Adapting a general-purpose AI to specialized fields stays hard and expensive.
approach Extract knowledge from pretraining, tune how much of that knowledge helps ranking in each domain, and keep everything stable across different field types without retraining.
Query Performance Prediction under Corpus Growth in Dense Retrieval
SIGIR
problem As search databases grow larger, the quality of existing speed-estimation methods breaks down, making it impossible to predict if a query will work well.
approach Adapt existing speed-estimation methods to account for growing databases and test whether they predict performance loss as databases expand.

SIGIR
problem Evaluating whether an AI correctly sources its facts requires judging answer quality, but the field doesn't know if AI-written judgments match human judgment.
approach Compare how humans and AI systems grade the same answers, then test which automatic grading methods humans actually agree with.

SIGIR
problem When asking large AI systems to rank search results best-first, their rankings vary wildly depending on how the question is phrased, making them unreliable in production.
approach Test whether ranking quality stays consistent when changing the instruction from 'put best first' to 'put best last' to understand brittleness.

SIGIR
problem Modern AI search has three breakpoints—finding docs, generating answers, and judging quality—but it's unclear if the same queries fail at all three.
approach Analyze four years of benchmark data to see where difficulty comes from in each stage and test if fixing one stage alone helps.

SIGIR
problem Re-ranking search results costs money and time, but systems default to checking the same number of candidates for every query regardless of need.
approach Learn from past queries which ones need deeper checking, then predict depth per query to cut costs while keeping quality.

SIGIR
problem Search engines struggle to find exactly what you want because they either guess poorly from your words alone or they need expensive AI to rephrase your query better.
approach Combine an old, fast search method with a new AI trick: the AI writes fake documents that could answer you, then the fast method learns which terms matter most in those fake answers.

SIGIR
problem When a database searches for buildings in a certain neighborhood, it wastes time checking points outside the area, slowing down the answer.
approach Use a smarter way to divide the search area into chunks so fewer irrelevant points are checked, speeding up the query by roughly 3.6 times.

SIGIR
problem Finding two nearly identical documents in a massive collection takes forever because existing systems waste effort checking documents that are clearly too different.
approach Use tighter limits (checking length first) to skip non-matching documents much faster, shrinking the work load by up to 58 times.

SIGIR

approach Blend two trained versions of the model (one for chat, one for single queries) without retraining, so it works well for both without forgetting either.
Semantic Recall for Vector Search
SIGIR
problem Tests that measure how well a search system finds nearby items penalize it for missing items that aren't actually relevant, even though it found the right ones.
approach Measure only whether the system finds the items that truly matter semantically, ignoring false penalties for missing irrelevant neighbors.

SIGIR

SIGIR

SIGIR

SIGIR

Stage III · 412 papers

Anticipate the want

The problem. Often people don't search at all — they scroll, and expect the right thing to already be there. The system has to guess what someone wants before they ask, using nothing but their past behavior and everyone else's. Guess well and it feels like magic; guess badly and it's noise — and it must keep working for brand-new people and brand-new items it has never seen before.

The approach. Learn from the crowd — if people like you liked something, you probably will too — and increasingly from the order and timing of what each person did, to catch a shifting mood. This is where the field touches the business of the web most directly: the same machinery that recommends what to watch also places the ads that pay for it.

Recommender Systems 336

Problem. With millions of items and no search query, the system has to guess what each person wants from behavior alone — and keep working for brand-new people and items it's never seen. Approach. Learn from the whole crowd (people like you liked this) and from the order and timing of each person's actions, to predict the next thing they'll want.

WWW
problem When recommending products, combining images and text usually gives little new insight, and the system misses that collaborative meaning (what users typically buy) differs from visual meaning, confusing the recommendation.
approach Learn three separate views—collaborative purchase patterns, item links, and visual patterns—then align them by maximizing how consistent they are with each other.

WWW

approach Extract knowledge from descriptions of each image using an AI system and teach the model using two teacher signals that guide how text and images align together.

Stage IV · 367 papers

Understand and answer

The problem. For decades these systems handed back a list of links and left the understanding to you. The new expectation is that the machine reads your need in plain language, reasons about it, and returns an actual answer — drawn from the organized world, and correct. That asks for something the field didn't used to have: real command of language, and a way to keep the answer tied to fact rather than confidently made up.

The approach. This is where large language models — systems trained on enormous amounts of text that can read and write fluently — have swept in, and they now reach into nearly every corner of the field. The main trick for keeping them honest is to let them look things up first and answer only from what they found; the frontier is chaining several such steps into systems that plan and act on their own. (The look-it-up-first pattern is called retrieval-augmented generation; the act-on-their-own systems, agents.)

LLMs & Language Models 61

Problem. A system meant to understand a person has to actually grasp language — its ambiguity, its context, the endless ways to say the same thing — which the old keyword-matching approaches never truly did. Approach. Use models trained on enormous amounts of human writing, which have soaked up how language works, then adapt and steer them toward a particular job.

problem When people have back-and-forth conversations with AI search assistants, there's no good way to fairly grade whether the AI is doing a good job without expensive human reviewers.
approach Break down each conversation into small pieces and score them one by one, then combine those scores to judge overall quality in a way that matches how humans actually evaluate conversations.
Seeing the Whole Through the Parts: Discovering Objects through Semantic Part Mining in Weak Supervision
SIGIR
problem Photos tagged with only an object name stay ambiguous: Is the camera showing part of the table or the whole table, and which bits matter?
approach Spot the individual parts that make up an object (legs, seat, back for a chair), then assemble them to figure out the complete thing, copying how humans see.
Mitigating Evidence Suppression: Bi-level Active Evidence Injection for Educational Video Understanding
SIGIR
problem Vision-language AI ignores key diagrams in educational videos because other patterns grab attention first, causing wrong answers despite evidence being present.
approach Boost the importance of evidence tokens during decoding by training a lightweight module to pick which evidence to amplify and how much.
SciCheck: Reasoning Distillation for Biomedical Claim Verification
SIGIR
problem Checking if medical claims are true requires finding evidence online, but smaller AI systems can't do this efficiently without expensive gold-labeled data.
approach Train a small AI on reasoning steps from a bigger AI, mixing web evidence with perfect data, so it fact-checks without huge compute.
Tokens to Types: Context Editing with Selective Entity Abstraction for Grounded Generation
SIGIR
problem AI systems often repeat things they memorized during training instead of reading the context you give them. When the context contradicts training data (like a fictional company or updated facts), the AI ignores it and hallucinates wrong answers.
approach Replace the real names of people and places that appear in both the context and the question with generic labels (City-1, Company-2), stripping away the AI's memorized biases while keeping the logical relationships intact.

SIGIR
problem Privacy policies are unstructured text, making it nearly impossible to check whether a company collects only the personal data it really needs.
approach Extract what data is collected and why, compare it to patterns in well-written policies, then flag cases where collection seems unnecessarily broad.

SIGIR
problem Detecting AI-written peer reviews assumes someone either wrote it or an AI did, missing the messy reality where people and AI work together on the same review.
approach Created a benchmark with human reviews, fully AI-generated reviews, and mixed reviews where ideas came from humans but writing from AI, to see if detectors conflate authorship with actual contribution.

LLM Agents & Tool Use 100

Problem. A model that only produces text can answer a question but can't *do* anything — run the query, check the database, book the thing. Real tasks take several steps, decisions, and actions in the world. Approach. Let the model plan a sequence of steps and reach for outside tools between them — a search, a calculator, a database — so it can act, see what happened, and adjust. A worker, not just an oracle.

Retrieval-Augmented (RAG) 129

Problem. A language model only knows what it absorbed while training; ask it something recent or specific and it will answer confidently and be wrong. Approach. Before it answers, have it look the facts up in a trusted source and write its reply from what it found — anchoring the fluent guesser in real evidence.

approach Train the system in stages to detect when it senses conflicting information by comparing confidence across sources, use that signal to select better training examples and prefer robust choices.

Stage V · 230 papers

Guard the commons

The problem. Everything above is powerful and consequential, and the web is adversarial. The same systems that recommend and answer can also discriminate, leak private data, spread lies, or be gamed by fraudsters — and when a system decides who sees what, being unfair or wrong has real human cost. A field that shapes what billions of people read can't only ask does it work; it has to ask is it fair, private, honest, and safe.

The approach. A whole counter-current of the research is defensive: catching fraud and coordinated abuse, spotting false information, protecting privacy (including learning from data without ever gathering it all in one place), measuring and removing unfair bias, and making a system's decisions clear enough to trust. A related thread asks not just what predicts an outcome but what actually causes it — the difference between a coincidence and a lever you can safely pull.

Fairness, Bias & Responsible AI 8

Problem. A system trained on human data inherits human prejudice, and when it decides who sees a job ad or gets a loan, that bias becomes real discrimination at scale. Approach. Measure where outcomes come out unfairly across groups of people, and adjust the data or the model so it treats them evenhandedly instead of quietly encoding old biases.

approach Looks at meaningful clusters of pixels rather than tiny patches, focusing attention only on the specific style details the user cares about.

problem Short videos spread lies fast and in many ways—some are manipulated, some are deepfakes, some are old clips used wrong—but current tools only catch one type.
approach Build a system that looks at the video's content and fact-checks against real sources to spot which specific kind of manipulation is being used.

WWW
problem Clickbait headlines trick AI systems into thinking false stories are true because AI tends to go along with what people believe, not what's actually real.
approach Use AI to argue both for and against a headline, then train a smaller AI to spot when the arguments disagree with what's actually verified.
HierCon: Hierarchical Contrastive Attention for Audio Deepfake Detection
WWW
problem AI deepfake voices sound real now, but spotting them requires looking at how layers of learned sound patterns work together—and current tools ignore that layering.
approach Treat the layers as connected layers of clues and look at how they relate to each other, like reading a story where each line makes the next make sense.

WWW
problem AI doesn't know when it's confused, when it's making things up, or when the question is out of its depth—so people can't tell when to trust it.
approach Give AI systems a way to track their own confidence and uncertainty, then automatically switch to slower, more careful thinking for hard questions.

WWW

approach Have different agents look at different parts of the evidence to catch the weak misleading signals, then combine their findings smartly.

WWW

Fraud & Anomaly Detection 63

Problem. Among billions of ordinary actions, a tiny fraction are fraud, spam, or abuse — and the people behind them constantly change tactics to blend in. Approach. Learn what normal looks like well enough that the rare, coordinated, or out-of-pattern behavior stands out on its own, even as the patterns keep shifting.

VarParser: Unleashing the Neglected Power of Variables for LLM-based Log Parsing
WWW
problem Engineers parse massive log files to find errors, but AI systems focus only on the fixed parts and ignore the changing parts, wasting computation and losing visibility into what actually varied.
approach Flip to focus on the changing parts, use them to group logs more efficiently, and avoid redundant AI calls by remembering past decisions on the same changing parts.
Revisiting Graph-Level Anomaly Detection: From Partially to Fully Unsupervised Learning
WWW
problem Systems that flag unusual graphs (frauds, botnets) train only on normal examples, but in reality the training data contains anomalies anyway, creating a gap between theory and practice.
approach Model uncertainty as a distribution over predictions so anomalies have less influence during training, and iteratively separate normal from abnormal without expert filtering.
Multi-Modal Enhanced Graph Transfer Learning for Digital Finance Fraud Detection
WWW
problem Cryptocurrency fraud spreads across multiple blockchains in complex ways, and catching it is hard because blockchain text, timing, and network structure matter together.
approach Combine graph structure, time patterns, and text metadata to spot when fraud tactics learned on one blockchain apply to a new one.
Dir-GD: Directed Graph Distillation
WWW
problem Social networks and transaction graphs with billions of connections are too huge to train AI systems on, but shrinking them loses directional meaning (like who paid whom) that matters for fraud detection.
approach Split a giant directed graph into smaller independent pieces using community detection, train on the pieces separately, then merge them into a tiny synthetic graph that keeps the original's direction and structure.
Cross-Type Semantic Alignment for Multi-Type Anomaly Detection in Heterogeneous Graphs
WWW
problem Fraud and crime networks in the real world have many different types of actors, but systems that learn from connections struggle when some actor types are much sparser or more important than others.
approach They align how suspicious patterns spread across different actor types, then use separate learning streams that protect each type's distinct task without conflicting with the others.

WWW
problem Fake reviews and click fraud happen in organized gangs that hide their abuse by acting collectively, evading normal detection.
approach They treat spam as a group problem, not individual behavior, and use a single learning process to catch the coordinated patterns.

WWW
problem When data is stored separately (no single company holds everything), it's vulnerable to saboteurs who poison part of the data.
approach They show how to attack those systems and then defend them by spotting when one location's data looks wrong compared to the others.

Explainability & Interpretability 6

Problem. The most accurate systems are black boxes — they hand you an answer but not a reason — and you can't trust, fix, or be accountable for a decision you can't understand. Approach. Causal Inference 4

Problem. Data is full of things that happen together, but acting on a coincidence that isn't a cause leads you astray — copying what people who quit happened to click won't stop anyone quitting. Approach. Separate what merely comes *with* an outcome from what actually *drives* it, so a system can predict the effect of an action it has never tried.

Stage VI · 23 papers

Do it at scale

The problem. None of this matters if it can't run for billions of people, on real machines, right now. A method that's accurate but too slow or too heavy to deploy is a paper, not a product — and the newest language models are enormous. So a constant pressure runs underneath everything: make it smaller, faster, cheaper, without losing what made it good.

The approach. Shrink and speed up — compress big models, teach small ones to imitate large ones, cut the work each request costs — and build systems that spread across many machines. It's the least glamorous stage and the one that decides whether any of the rest ever reaches a person.

Efficiency & Scalability 18

Problem. A method that's brilliant but too slow, too large, or too costly to run for billions of people in real time never leaves the lab — and the newest models are enormous. Approach. Shrink and speed things up — compress big models, teach small ones to imitate large ones, cut the work each request costs — without giving up what made them good.

approach Automatically try hundreds of different cleaning and chunking combinations and learn which one works best for each dataset, without manual guessing.
Confidence-Based Stopping Methods for Systematic Reviews
SIGIR
problem Medical reviews search thousands of papers to answer a specific question, but reviewers read far more documents than needed if the answer has already emerged clearly.
approach Stop screening documents once evidence is strong enough to make a confident decision, rather than reading everything available.
Connecting the dots

One pipeline, one layer

Lay the six stages end to end and the whole conference is a single pipeline: the field has to organize the world's information, then handle the everyday acts of search and recommendation, increasingly understand and answer in natural language, guard against the adversaries, and do all of it at scale. Each stage exists because the one before it left a problem unsolved — you can't search or recommend what you haven't organized, you can't answer before you've searched, you can't trust an answer that isn't defended.

And one change cuts across all six. The oldest data-mining systems were built from explicit rules and hand-tuned statistics — if this pattern appears, do that. Over the last decade, that layer was replaced by learned representations: we stopped writing rules and started teaching systems to learn patterns from examples. This year, one tool shows up in every stage: large language models, systems trained on enormous text that can read, reason, and write fluently. They now touch search, recommendation, entities, graphs, generation, defense, and cost. The single most-common research move across these 1487 papers is to ask: "what if we let an LLM see this problem?"

Data mining has always been the art of connecting a person to information they need out of too much information they don't. Its 2026 turn is the same art learning to read fluently and answer directly — statistics and search and the shapes of graphs still at the core, but a machine that increasingly understands language and can reason about what it finds.

All 1487 WWW, WSDM, and SIGIR 2026 technical papers (from official proceedings; 100% with abstracts), each read for its contribution by a language model; stages and framing written from first principles. Browse/search them in the explorer · overview in the landscape · see how the venues differ.
